Five-fold evaluation on 1,846 devices shows top-ten selection raises mean power conversion efficiency (PCE) from 19.68\% to 23.74\%. Three learned policies are compared with six baselines under 3,000 surrogate evaluations per run. In top-100 shortlists, Proximal policy optimization (PPO) and recurrent PPO (RPPO) achieve neighborhood-supported hit rates of 23\% and 24\% (7.9- and 8.3-fold Pure Random's 2.9\%), versus 10\% (3.4-fold) for the baselines with the highest supported hit rate (L-SHADE and Ax BO, tied) at $K = 100$. Gains coexist with weak-support fractions. Of 9,000 recipes, 65.4\% match a composition family at similarity 0.80, falling to 11.7\% at 0.90. Results support enrichment-oriented prioritization but not experimental performance, seed robustness, or matched-budget advantage.


### [50] [Learning nonlinear interactions and cross-feature couplings with product-unit residual networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18074)
*Ziyuan Li, Uwe Jaekel, Babette Dellen*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Understanding nonlinear feature interactions is crucial in science and engineering, yet standard multilayer perceptrons (MLPs) often capture such interactions only implicitly, leading to entangled representations that can impair robustness and interpretability. We investigate complex-valued product-unit residual networks (CV-PURe) that integrate multiplicative product units with residual connections to explicitly model cross-feature couplings while stabilizing optimization. We conduct a systematic evaluation on an interaction-driven synthetic benchmark and real-world datasets and evaluate prediction together with input-level interaction recovery under a reproducible protocol. Across five data splits and five training seeds, CV-PURe achieves the lowest mean RMSE on four of six scientific benchmarks, reducing Friedman error by 53.9% relative to MLP. Its Friedman interaction maps assign 60.7% of total strength to the known interacting pair, compared with 31.0% for MLP. On a synthetic task containing third- and fourth-order products and 20 distractors, CV-PURe reduces RMSE from 0.1376 to 0.0797 relative to a complex-valued MLP and recovers all nine true pairwise projections in every training/sampling repetition. Bike Sharing experiments connect improved prediction to the contribution of temporal, calendar, and seasonal context through paired ablations of both PURe variants. Parkinsons Telemonitoring extends the context analysis to repeated measurements. Noise and learning-curve evaluations demonstrate sustained predictive gains under perturbed inputs and limited supervision, while inference measurements quantify computational cost. This article extends our ICCS 2026 paper, Modeling Nonlinear Feature Interactions with Product-Unit Residual Networks (doi: https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-032-29921-5_6 ).


### [51] [Deep Latents, Long Memory: Latent persistence explains temporal correlations in transformer activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18089)
*Francesco Cagnetta, Santiago Acevedo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: The hidden layers of Large Language Models (LLMs) are believed to encode representations of the data whose abstraction increases with depth. However, in the absence of an operational notion of abstraction, this statement is hard to test quantitatively. Here, we propose an alternative view, building on the principle that the correlations of a sequence reflect the structure of the process that generated it. We define the autocorrelation function of a layer as the average dot product between its activations at two context positions separated by a lag. Latent variables encoded persistently along the context create slowly decaying tails in the autocorrelation, and we find these tails to grow across layers in both synthetic and natural data. In synthetic data with controlled hierarchical latent structure, this growth stems from the encoding of abstract latent variables, which govern long stretches of the input. Indeed, the persistence of latent codes predicts the qualitative structure of autocorrelations and accounts for most of the empirical measures. In real text, we take sparse-autoencoder features as proxies for latent variables, and find that their persistence correlates strongly with the magnitude of autocorrelations across datasets and LLMs. In particular, the persistence time increases with depth, as do autocorrelations, implying that deeper latent features persist for longer context spans. We complement this analysis by explicitly ablating the persistent sparse-autoencoder features and observing a consequent decrease in the autocorrelations.


### [52] [Efficient Poincaré Convolutions through Patch-Statistics Factorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18125)
*Lukáš Jochymek, Markéta Vašinková, Petr Gajdoš*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Published implementations of the Poincaré convolution in Hyperbolic Neural Networks++ (HNN++) map every overlapping image patch to the Poincaré ball before evaluating its score. We show that the clipped score depends on each patch only through two statistics, its squared norm and its inner products with the filters, which a native convolution and a single-channel box filter compute directly. The factorized layer therefore evaluates the specified score exactly, without constructing ball-valued patches, and its single-precision outputs and gradients agree with double-precision evaluations at least as closely as those of the explicit reference. On an RTX A6000 under two versions of JAX, complete training updates of a Poincaré U-Net and a Poincaré ResNet-20 are 1.5 to 1.8 times faster than the faster of two explicit implementations that map patches to the ball (1.2 to 1.4 times against an explicit implementation that skips this mapping); on a V100, the gain over the gather-based one persists as the ResNet-20 is widened eightfold. In 15 paired training runs per task, updates are 2.0 and 1.8 times faster than a reference that follows the published execution order, with no consistent change in final segmentation or classification quality, and peak memory falls by 38 to 55% in a U-Net scaling study. Replacing the convolutions of the official PyTorch Hyperbolic U-Net makes its training step 1.4 times faster with half the memory. Evaluating an existing layer from its patch statistics thus makes Poincaré convolutional networks cheaper to train in the tested models and software stacks.


### [53] [When Copying Is Hard to Beat: A Negative Result in Clinical Assessment Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18142)
*Marius Fracarolli, Stefan Riezler*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We report a negative result in next-day clinical assessment forecasting: the evaluated models show no consistent advantage over strong persistence-based baselines. Clinicians can view the previous day's questionnaire answers, a plausible source of documentation persistence. Predicting 45 items on 29,715 ICU day-pairs with patient-disjoint splits, a physiology-informed gated model improves item-macro-F1 over a one-line clinical rule by +0.0019 [+0.0006,+0.0031], while losing accuracy. No macro-F1 advantage is established when the physiology window ends at the previous form's reference time (+0.0001 [-0.0006,+0.0008]), or when scoring excludes the 68.5% of cells derived from parent answers; combining both changes yields a small disadvantage. Four checkpoints across three families underperform persistence, and a fine-tuned one differs from it on only two cells. A categorical read-out raises changed-cell accuracy eightfold, but a state-only read-out scores higher overall than forecast-only decoding and matches a transition matrix. This negative result highlights how the documentation process, input cutoff, observation mask and decoding rule shape conclusions about forecasting recorded assessments.


### [54] [STAMP: Manufacturing-Informed Generative Design and Shape Optimization of Sheet Panels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18144)
*Jihoon Kim, Jun Hyeok Song, Wonho Ha, Namwoo Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Generative design of formed sheet panels must preserve geometric manufacturing conditions while exploring layouts and improving performance. STAMP embeds these conditions directly in the shape representation. Each panel is described by its material layout and a reference surface with one height at each occupied position in a plane perpendicular to the drawing direction. Nominal thickness is prescribed along the normal. The representation allows curved surfaces, ribs, and openings. It excludes reference-surface undercuts and keeps nominal thickness fixed without manufacturing penalty terms. Because these conditions belong to the representation, every generated and optimized panel satisfies them regardless of training-set size. Boundary-conditioned diffusion generates alternative layouts, and predictor gradients guide a surface optimization that retains outlines, openings, and designated fixed regions. We demonstrate STAMP on automotive hood inner panels using CarHoods10k. With a comparable number of stored values, depth maps reconstruct surfaces more accurately than point-cloud and voxel baselines. From only five training designs, one of three training runs yields novel and varied layouts. A generator trained on 500 designs, 6.5% of the training set, already reaches the layout variety of the full-data model. Across 11 held-out design families, predictor-based selection among existing dataset designs yields an average gap of 0.18 mm to the best available FEA deformation, versus an expected 1.59 mm for random selection. It does better than random selection in all 11 families. At a 10% depth-change bound, a network not used in the search predicts an average deformation reduction of 24.8% for six generated and existing panels, with a 3.2% average mass increase computed from geometry. Engineers can thus explore and optimize panels within this manufacturing-informed design space.


### [55] [Which Model, For Which Variable? Per-Variable Forecast Combination in Sparse ICU Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18149)
*Marius Fracarolli, Stefan Riezler*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Clinical forecasting papers typically report a single best architecture. We show that on multivariate ICU physiology this question conceals variable-specific differences: no architecture is best on every variable, and which model wins changes from variable to variable. Across five ICU databases (MIMIC-III, MIMIC-IV, eICU, HiRID, and UMM (University Medicine Mannheim), a German single-center ICU) we forecast 94 channels over a 24-hour horizon with a pool of 14 models, and combine them with weights fitted per variable. On test, the combination beats the best single model on all five databases by 6.1-11.0% mean squared error (mean 8.7%). The gain is concentrated where clinical data is thinnest: the sparsest third of channels improves by 11.9% and laboratory values by 7.5%, against 3.1% for the densest third. Rank-based harmonic weighting of the best five models per variable, with no fitted weights at all, recovers about four-fifths of the gain. Extending the pool afterwards with a per-channel gradient-boosted tree, ARIMA and ETS makes the tree the validation-selected best single model on all five databases; per-variable combination still improves on it by 2.9-5.9% (significant on four of five), while equal weighting of the same members does not. As on univariate benchmarks, learned combination beats simple averaging - but only once the weights are constrained. We also report which model to prefer when, and show that the ordering changes with database size.


### [56] [On the Limits of Sufficient Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18163)
*Klaus Weinbauer, Peter F. Stadler, Thomas Gärtner, Sagar Malhotra*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We formally characterize the conditions under which sufficient explanations exist for classifiers on structured data such as graphs and strings. We focus on post-hoc explainers that select parts of the input as sufficient explanations. An explanation is sufficient if the selected part alone ensures the predicted label. Sufficient explanations have been widely adopted across data modalities, including graphs, strings, and images. However, a unified formal analysis of sufficient explanations remains missing. We bridge this gap with four main contributions: (i) We define a general explanation formalism consisting of an instance space, a classifier, and a select relation. The select relation specifies which potential sub-instances or feature subsets can be selected as explanations from each instance; (ii) We show that the existence of sufficient explainers is fully characterized by the interplay between the classifier and the select relation; (iii) We extend this analysis to practically relevant variants of sufficiency, including probabilistic sufficiency and deletion sufficiency; (iv) Based on the formal analysis, we devise statistics that bound the maximum achievable sufficiency for a given task. We evaluate these statistics on language, protein, and molecular classification datasets and find that full sufficiency is unattainable on most of them. Since these bounds hold for every explainer, they provide a task-level reference against which any explainer's sufficiency can be judged in absolute terms.


### [57] [MarathonTS: a benchmark of long synthetic time series from window assembly and long-series generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18190)
*Ilyas Varshavskiy, Sergey Shulgin, Shuhrat Khalilbekov, Bonu Boboeva, Azizjon Azimi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Many applications of synthetic time series require a series that is much longer than the windows on which a generative model is trained. Such a series can be assembled from a pool of short generated windows, which requires deciding which windows are taken, in what order, and how their seams are processed, or it can be produced directly by a long-series generator. This study presents MarathonTS, to our knowledge the first open benchmark that compares both families under one protocol. Every method produces a series of the same length on 9 open hourly datasets with 10 seeds. The series is evaluated by its fidelity to the real data (83 metrics in 12 groups, against three real references: train, validation and an out-of-time split), by its utility (models are trained on the synthetic series only and tested on real data, TSTR against TRTR, in 14 classification and regression tasks), by its memorisation of the training data and by its computational cost. The statistical analysis is carried out as a separate stage that reads the published tables and gives the rankings of every method in every slice, the answers to the research questions with tests and uncertainty, and the results of a pre-specified confirmatory family. In the main run, the methods differ in both confirmatory cells; the choice of windows matters more than the processing of their seams, which does not improve the series, and no method trains the downstream models as well as the real series. The top tier of the leaderboard is reproduced on other hardware, whereas the exact places within it are not. For the reproducibility of the study, the code, the data hashes and all results are publicly available (links below).


### [58] [T2ST: Data-Efficient Spatio-Temporal Foundation Models via Temporal Data and Knowledge Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18199)
*Yue Xu, Kaiyun Wei, Wenying Duan, Zichen Xu, Shuai Ma, Xiaoxi He*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Spatio-temporal foundation models (STFMs) require large-scale training corpora, yet synchronized observations and spatial metadata are costly to acquire and curate, limiting the availability of suitable data. By contrast, time-series data are abundant and easier to collect. Since observations at each location form a time series, this motivates a natural question: can abundant time-series data enhance STFMs when spatio-temporal observations are scarce? To bridge this gap, we propose T2ST, a data-efficient training framework that augments limited spatio-temporal observations with selected auxiliary time series and transfers knowledge from a frozen time-series foundation model. Our key insight is that temporal pattern learning can benefit from abundant external time series, while spatial dependency learning remains grounded in real spatio-temporal observations. Guided by this insight, T2ST retrieves matched sequences to reinforce sparsely observed temporal patterns and selects reliable complementary sequences to broaden temporal coverage. Temporal relation distillation aligns relationships among historical patches, while multi-scale prediction distillation transfers forecasting behavior at fine and coarse temporal resolutions, enabling knowledge transfer across heterogeneous architectures. Using only 10\% of the original spatio-temporal data, T2ST enables STFMs to achieve overall forecasting performance competitive with or superior to their full-data counterparts across six long-term and twelve short-term forecasting datasets. These results establish a new paradigm for data-efficient training of spatio-temporal foundation models. Code is available at this https URL .


### [59] [NeoResearch: Literature- and Experience-Guided Autonomous Time Series Model Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18235)
*Ze Guo, Mingyue Cheng, Xiaoyu Tao, Shuo Yu, Shangtong Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Developing effective forecasting models for diverse time-series domains requires substantial expert knowledge, evidence from scientific literature on the web, and iterative experimentation. However, translating this knowledge and research experience into model designs requires preserving source context and testing applicability under task-specific assumptions. In this paper, we propose NeoResearch, a literature- and experience-guided autonomous research framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) as researchers to discover lightweight, domain-specific forecasting models. Specifically, NeoResearch establishes an expert-inspired dual-loop research workflow, coupling hypothesis-driven architecture exploration with iterative training and hyperparameter optimization through experimental feedback. To enable knowledge-grounded discovery, the agent autonomously mines scientific literature from the Web, extracting modeling mechanisms, empirical evidence, and applicability conditions to formulate task-grounded, testable architecture hypotheses. Furthermore, a hierarchical experience memory links literature findings to experimental outcomes and consolidates conditional lessons, guiding architecture discovery and training optimization while supporting reuse across forecasting tasks. Experiments on ten datasets show competitive performance and support dual loops, literature extraction, and memory reuse. Our code is available.


### [60] [Small Is Not Dead: Distance-Aware Cluster Revival for e-means](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18238)
*Eyal Weiss*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: e-means (Bessa et al., 2026) is a recent mini-batch k-means algorithm, now part of the FAISS library, that reaches the error of Lloyd's algorithm about an order of magnitude faster on large embedding datasets. Every five mini-batches, it removes every cluster whose running point count falls below a tenth of the average and reuses its centre to split a large cluster. In the authors' ablation, removing this mechanism, called dead-cluster revival, raises the error more than removing any other component added to the moving-average update. The rule, however, considers only cluster size: it cannot distinguish a cluster that is small because it is redundant from one that covers a rare, isolated group of points, and on data with such groups it repeatedly removes centres whose removal is costly. We add a second condition, based on the distance between centres: a small cluster is removed only if its centre is close to another centre, relative to a running estimate of the mean squared error. When a centre equals the mean of its points, this squared distance is an upper bound on the average increase in squared error, per point of the cluster, caused by removing the centre. The condition costs one nearest-centre search for the small clusters every five mini-batches and adds no measurable running time. On the e-means paper's embedding benchmarks, it never prevents a removal, so the results are identical to those of FAISS. On datasets with small isolated groups not used during development, it reduces the test error after two epochs by roughly 10% to 80% relative to count-based revival, and it preserves the advantage of k-means++ initialization; on the other evaluation datasets, it stays within about 1% of the better of revival enabled and disabled. We also report two failure modes that the analysis predicts. This is a CPU proof of concept; GPU implementations are left to future work.


### [61] [Neural Classifiers as Error-Correcting Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18239)
*Sergey Salishev, Anton Makarov, Oleg Granichin, Renata Avros, Zeev Volkovich*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: A unified framework is proposed that characterizes supervised deep classification as the decoding of error-correcting codes in the Shannon sense. Cross-entropy optimization is shown to align softmax classifiers with maximum-likelihood decoding, where logits map to Hamming distances to class-specific codewords. This correspondence explains the emergence of standard divergence objectives (e.g., Kullback-Leibler, Jeffreys) and provides a geometric basis for the stability of decision boundaries. Furthermore, the geometries of Neural Collapse and LeJEPA are identified as optimal constellations for decoding under isotropic noise. Unlike classical Error-Correcting Output Codes, deep networks are shown to learn effective codebooks in the feature space implicitly. This insight is leveraged to derive novel criteria for lossless quantization, improved knowledge distillation, and architectural regularization based on code redundancy. Finally, this perspective is extended to self-supervised encoders, offering a cohesive explanation for the geometry of latent representations in modern neural models.


### [62] [ParetoM$^3$: Learning on the Pareto Set under Preference Guidance via Min-Max-Min Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18252)
*Pei Tang, Songtao Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Learning across multiple inherently conflicting objectives requires selecting desirable trade-offs from the Pareto set. In this work, we study preference-guided multi-objective optimization, where a predefined preference function is minimized over the weak Pareto set of multiple nonconvex objectives. Leveraging a merit function that vanishes on the weak Pareto set, we propose a penalty-based min-max-min formulation, termed the M$^3$ problem, which unifies preference optimization and the enforcement of weak Pareto optimality in a single objective. We further develop ParetoM$^3$, a single-loop first-order algorithm for computing stationary solutions of the resulting M$^3$ problem. Under a local Kurdyka-Lojasiewicz assumption, we prove that ParetoM$^3$ finds an $\epsilon$-stationary point of the M$^3$ problem that is also approximately weakly Pareto optimal for the original multi-objective problem when the penalty parameter is sufficiently large, with explicit non-asymptotic convergence rates. We also show that this stationarity notion recovers existing optimality criteria, including approximate preference stationarity and approximate Karush-Kuhn-Tucker conditions, under the same assumptions used in prior works. Experiments on a synthetic benchmark, image classification, regression, and math reasoning demonstrate that ParetoM$^3$ reliably attains preferred weak Pareto optimal solutions while achieving strong practical performance. Code is available at this https URL .


### [63] [Right Token, Right Time: From Per-Token Gating to Trajectory-Aware Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18274)
*Nima Eshraghi, Lovedeep Gondara, Sagarika Suresh, Xiaotong Xu, Jithin Pradeep, Fanny Chevalier*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Activation steering via sparse autoencoders (SAEs) enables behavioral control of large language models without task-specific fine-tuning. Yet, standard methods apply the steering signal at every generated token, displacing representations from the distribution the model was trained on and eroding generation quality. We show that \emph{when} along a generation the intervention is applied is a decisive axis of control. We cast steering as a per-token allocation problem under a fixed budget of steerable tokens, so that methods differ only in which tokens they select, making allocation the sole variable. Within this framework we introduce two gates. The first, Contrastive Feature Projection (CFP), is memoryless and selects the tokens least aligned with the target behavior through a sparse, interpretable feature projection. The second, Closed-Loop Adaptive Bernoulli Steering (CLABS), is trajectory aware. It treats allocation as closed-loop feedback control, tracking the alignment a generation has accumulated so far and spending budget where the trajectory falls behind. On a linear steering-response model we derive an explicit gain region that guarantees closed-loop stability of CLABS and show that its integral term reduces the steady-state tracking offset. Empirically, CFP and CLABS achieve the largest behavioral shift among budgeted gates at matched budgets, with CLABS strongest under the tight budgets where allocation matters most, while best preserving fluency.


### [64] [Clean Tickets, Wrong Orders: Catching Silent Errors with a Language Model in Your Browser Tab](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18284)
*Umar Ahmed, Maxim Dunaev, Mohammad Nour Al Awad, Sergey Ivanov, Ivan Khodnenko*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: A small language model that turns a typed order into structure can fail in two ways. It can print something broken, which any system rejects. Or it can print a clean, well formed order that nobody placed. Our companion work calls the second kind a silent error, and it is the dangerous one. We present a web demo in which a fine-tuned 0.8B parameter model runs entirely inside the visitor's browser tab, through this http URL compiled to WebAssembly. No order text leaves the device. The page parses orders as the visitor types, scores every ticket with the PIZZA benchmark's own matcher ported to TypeScript, refuses anything off the menu with "?" so the model can face the open web, and lets visitors hunt for silent errors or try to cause one. Run in the browser on all 1,357 PIZZA test orders, the deployed model writes the same output as the server evaluation for 99.0% of them and reaches the same exact match (0.882); we also report its latency on 3 consumer laptops. Demo: this https URL


### [65] [Emulating physics-based XCO2 retrievals across OCO-2 viewing modes with compact neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18333)
*Xianlei San, Chengxing Zhai, Hui Su, Kuai Dai, Shuangchen Du*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Operational full-physics retrieval of the column-averaged dry-air mole fraction of carbon dioxide from satellite spectra is computationally demanding. We use singular value decomposition to compress spectra from the Orbiting Carbon Observatory-2 separately by band. Compact multilayer perceptrons use the resulting spectral scores, prior information, and viewing geometry to predict the difference between the full-physics retrieval and its operational prior. Adding the predicted difference to the prior yields an estimate of the retrieved mole fraction. The models were trained on 2015-2019 observations and validated on 2020 observations. In 2021-2025 tests, the mean annual root-mean-square errors relative to the Atmospheric Carbon Dioxide Observations from Space retrieval product were 0.89, 1.35, and 1.43 parts per million for ocean glint, land glint, and nadir observations, respectively. On East Asian nadir observations screened using the published criteria for the spectral Transformer, our model yielded a lower mean annual error than reported for that model in 2020-2022. These results show that compact neural networks using compressed spectra can efficiently emulate the full-physics retrieval across multiple viewing modes and years.


### [66] [DECODE: Decomposable Environments for COntrolled Sequential-Decision Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18367)
*Diego Silva Prado, Bruno Caldas de Souza, Tiago Amaral Penteado de Jesus, Ricardo Guimarães Herrmann, Cristiano Breuel, Cristiano Bevitori Maffia De Oliveira*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: When a sequential decision system falls short of an optimum, its score does not reveal which modifications would improve its decisions. DECODE is a benchmark for testing such modifications through controlled experiments on planning and available information. Its consumer-credit collections generator provides five partially observed environments that share known dynamics matrices and vary a memory parameter and process noise. The experiments change selected inputs or planning rules while keeping the rest of the solver fixed. Each comparison specifies the solver's state inputs, response inputs and planning horizon. On the deterministic base environment, which was selected for a large gap between six-month and one-month planning with the hidden state, extending planning from one to six months increases the scores of two gradient-boosted solvers by 15 to 17 percentage points of the exact optimum, computed with the hidden state and borrower traits. Even at this horizon, the best graded solver, whose repayment response is fitted on logs, remains 15.6 points below that optimum. In a specified intervention sequence, three changes to its state inputs and their evaluation during search lower its score by 6.3 points, and replacing its fitted repayment model with the true response, evaluated with hidden borrower traits, then raises it by 15.6, a net recovery of nine of those points. Because the intermediate effects are conditional on the repayment model and the order of the changes, they do not uniquely assign the gap to separate causes. Within these limits, DECODE makes hypotheses about solver performance testable through specified interventions under known dynamics and explicit information conditions.


### [67] [f-Divergence Self-Play for Tabular Anomaly Detection via Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18370)
*Hoang Tran Vuong, Linh Ngo Van, Dang Nguyen, Thin Nguyen, Phuoc Nguyen, Mehrtash Harandi, Trung Le*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Anomaly detection in tabular data poses significant challenges due to heterogeneous feature types-mixing numerical, categorical, and textual attributes, which complicate learning meaningful representations of normality. Recent work has applied large language models (LLMs) to this problem by serializing table rows as text sequences, yet these approaches rely on one-shot supervised fine-tuning that offers limited signal to tighten the model's description of normality. We propose DiSPaT, a self-play fine-tuning framework that strengthens the model's understanding of normal data. Building on the theoretical foundation of f-divergence minimization, we derive a tight approximation connecting our training objective to reducing the distributional gap between real normal data and model-generated samples. DiSPaT operates through an alternating optimization: at each iteration, the current policy generates synthetic samples that serve as pseudo-anomalies, while a critic discriminator learns to distinguish these from real normal samples; this signal drives policy updates that progressively align the model distribution with the true normal-data distribution. Extensive experiments on diverse benchmarks demonstrate that DiSPaT consistently outperforms prior LLM-based methods, deep learning approaches, and classical unsupervised detectors for tabular anomaly detection.


### [68] [Where Tree Ensembles Decide: Structural Local Explanation with Decision Predicate Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18382)
*Sylvio Barbon Junior, Luca Longo*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Tree ensembles are among the most accurate models for tabular data, yet the computation behind a single prediction is spread over hundreds of decision rules. Feature attributions and counterfactuals explain such predictions through the influence of features or a change of the input, leaving open which of the thresholds applied by the trees a prediction responds to, while structural explanations compress these rules and lose the trees behind them. To address this gap, we propose a local Decision Predicate Graph (local DPG), which gathers the rules executed for a sample into a verifiable record of the computation, with a class support that reproduces every explained prediction. From these rules we select the critical predicate, the executed predicate whose crossing is estimated to move the model most strongly toward a chosen class, which offers the user a concrete threshold and the trees that share it, without an external explainer. Across 16 datasets and four ensemble families, it separates differently predicted neighbours better than random and nearest-threshold predicates. A single crossing recovers the true class in 46-69% of wrong predictions, against 29-50% when the same rule is applied to the feature ranked first by TreeSHAP, a measure close to the selection criterion, and on planted faults it finds the faulty threshold in 46-71% of the resulting errors, against 6-21% at random. Although training-free edits of these predicates did not reduce the error on unseen data, the critical predicate complements attributions and counterfactuals by naming where a prediction can be changed.


### [69] [WeatherDreamer: Learning a World Model from Multimodal Earth Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18393)
*Kate Duffy, Elise Penn, Tristan Ballard, August Posch, Thomas J. Vandal*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Machine learning weather models now rival operational global forecasts but still start from analyses produced by separate, expensive data assimilation (DA) systems, which for global models are typically updated every six hours. Observation-to-observation models avoid this dependence, but most omit the recursive filtering cycle that lets DA systems carry a background state through sparse, irregular observations. We introduce WeatherDreamer, a world model that learns this cycle end-to-end from 18 global observing systems, without reanalysis. It infers a latent state from observations, carries it forward hour by hour with learned dynamics, corrects it each hour with new observations, and can predict what any of its observing systems would measure at any location. At held-out stations, its ensembles beat NOAA's Global Ensemble Forecast System (GEFS) for temperature, dew point and wind out to 12 h and are better calibrated than GEFS and the ECMWF ensemble. Precipitation, cloud-top height and solar irradiance, never inputs at inference, are inferred with skill matching or exceeding operational products at short leads. Against the first guess of NOAA's Gridpoint Statistical Interpolation (GSI) DA system, WeatherDreamer's short-range departures are within 10% of GSI's at surface stations and within 25% for moisture-sensitive microwave channels, but up to five times larger for temperature-sounding channels. Never given observation errors, its learned update assumes errors close to known instrument and retrieval errors of microwave sounders and scatterometer winds. WeatherDreamer also separates viewing geometry from atmospheric state, recovering 83-96% of observed limb darkening and producing seamless nadir-view imagery. Producing an hourly global analysis in about 1.5 minutes on one GPU, WeatherDreamer shows that assimilation and forecasting can be learned together directly from observations.


### [70] [Deep Neural Networks Training Efficiency: Does Grokking Modify its Dynamics?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18396)
*Eduardo Cueto-Mendoza, John D. Kelleher*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: We study whether grokking, the delayed emergence of out-of-distribution generalization after apparent overfitting, alters training efficiency in deep neural networks, where efficiency is measured as accuracy per unit energy. Using two canonical settings, modular arithmetic and MNIST, we analyze two neural network models under 16-, 32-, and 64-bit precision and use Orthograd to test whether grokking produces noticeable deviations from the typically decreasing dynamics of training efficiency. We consider both short training runs of up to 500 epochs and long training runs of up to 20,000 epochs, and in both cases identify grokking as a sharp improvement in test accuracy after a sustained train-test gap. In the short-horizon experiments, applying Orthograd at progressively stronger levels during early training does not induce grokking, and efficiency remains smoothly decreasing. In the long-horizon experiments, Orthograd induces grokking, but the efficiency curves remain approximately monotonic and show no appreciable local increase at the grokking transition. These results suggest that, in our setting, grokking does not materially alter overall training-efficiency dynamics.


### [71] [Fuse-PINN: Combining Finite Element Methods and Physics-Informed Neural Networks to enable massively parallel simulations of non-linear PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18409)
*Stanislas Pamela, Nicolas Pailliez, Emmanuel Franck, Victor Michel-Dansac, Laurent Navoret, Vignesh Gopakumar*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Research in the domain of Physics-Informed Neural Networks applied to PDE modelling has advanced significantly in recent years, providing a wide ecosystem of novel methods. However, one frequent limitation is the inability of PINNs to scale out on High-Performance Computing architectures to address more ambitious challenges, particularly non-linear physics models with turbulent dynamics in large spatial domains. Typical studies restrain demonstrations to toy models with simple physics equations, few variables, and rarely any complex non-linear dynamics evolved over long timescales. Part of the AI community has even adopted the idea that "PINNs don't scale". This work proves otherwise. We present a mathematically robust formulation of Finite-Element-Method for Neural Networks (FEM-NN) implemented into a comprehensive, modular framework: Fuse-PINN. This formulation provides the basis for a sclable parallelisation of Physics-Informed Neural Networks on GPU-based HPC systems. Furthermore, an Energy Natural Gradient Descent optimiser is implemented to allow high-precision convergence. Fuse-PINN is demonstrated on physics models relevant to Magnetic Confinement Fusion, including non-linear convective models with fine-scale dynamics. Near-perfect scaling is achieved on multi-GPU runs with increasing problem size.


### [72] [PACT: A Decoder-Confidence-Guided Two-Round Receiver for Joint Channel and Phase-Noise Estimation in Sub-THz OFDM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18450)
*Ruilin Wang, Xiaodai Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Frequency-selective fading and oscillator phase noise (PN) jointly impair coded sub-THz OFDM reception. We propose a decoder-confidence-guided two-round receiver combining a {pilot-aware complex transformer} (PACT) estimator, banded linear minimum mean-square error equalization, and neural normalized min-sum decoding. PACT jointly estimates the frequency-domain channel and effective time-domain PN through interacting complex-valued branches. Decoder-derived pseudo-pilots and continuous constellation-aware confidence guide second-round refinement through PACT inputs and attention, with parameters shared across rounds. At $E_b/N_0=30$~dB, the PACT receiver achieves approximately $70\%$ and $57\%$ lower code-block error rates than the best-performing learning-based baseline in the two benchmark settings, respectively. Frozen-weight tests further demonstrate applicability across channel/PN models, carrier frequencies, and FFT/bandwidth configurations.


### [73] [Black-Box Uncertainty Quantification for Large Language Models via Ensemble-of-Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18453)
*Wang Ma, Debarun Bhattacharjya, Junkyu Lee, Nhan H Pham, Harsha Kokel, Qiang Ji*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Reliable use of large language models (LLMs) requires uncertainty estimates that separate ambiguity intrinsic to a prompt from genuine knowledge gaps. Bayesian and deep-ensemble methods deliver this aleatoric/epistemic decomposition in principle, but are computationally prohibitive at LLM scale and require white-box access. Existing black-box methods, in contrast, summarize to a single scalar that cannot make the distinction. We introduce a two-level ensemble that bridges this gap. Stochastic decoding of a fixed prompt forms the inner ensemble; meaning-preserving semantic perturbations of the prompt form the outer ensemble. Variability is measured in a continuous embedding space, and the law of total covariance gives an exact within-/between-perturbation decomposition to estimate aleatoric and epistemic uncertainty (AU/EU); it requires only black-box sampling access. On top of this estimator, we contribute three theoretical results that ground the framework: (i) a finite-sample bias identity for nested Monte Carlo with a closed-form bias-corrected epistemic estimator; (ii) a Bayesian bridge bounding the gap between perturbation-population AU/EU and Bayesian targets; and (iii) a separation result identifying a class of failure modes that any consistency-only black-box method provably misses while perturbation-based EU detects. Across five short QA benchmarks and four instruction-tuned LLMs spanning two families and two scales, our estimator is competitive with strong white-box baselines, and at a matched generation budget it matches leading black-box methods on error ranking while decomposing AU and EU; controlled paired interventions show that AU and EU respond selectively to distinct, actionable sources of failure: prompt ambiguity and model knowledge gaps; and a long-form pilot on the NQ long-answer split shows the framework extends to claim-level factuality without retraining.


### [74] [Sample-efficient prediction of high-entropy alloy strength through outcome-aware robust sequential learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18462)
*Ahmed Shoyeb Raihan, Md Habibor Rahman, Ashish Nimbarte, Imtiaz Ahmed*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Accurate prediction of high-entropy alloy (HEA) strength can guide composition and processing decisions; however, obtaining tensile-test data for HEAs can be costly and experimentally demanding. This study proposes Robust Outcome-aware Sequential Experimentation for High-Entropy Alloys (ROSE-HEA), a sample-efficient framework for predicting ultimate tensile strength (UTS) from alloy composition and processing conditions. Its acquisition policy anticipates how possible UTS outcomes update predictions and the support for complementary Gaussian process (GP) descriptions. It then reassesses the worst-case expected predictive risk remaining across a reference domain to select informative candidates. A retrospective evaluation on 501 HEA records compares ROSE-HEA with expected-improvement (EI) and upper-confidence-bound (UCB) Bayesian optimization (BO), random GP sampling, and eight conventional regressors across 30 matched runs with shared initial observations and test conditions. Starting from 25 initial observations and acquiring 100 additional responses, ROSE-HEA achieves the lowest mean root-mean-square error (RMSE), at 152.84 MPa, and the highest mean R^2, at 0.7443. Mean RMSE is reduced by 6.16% and 6.37% relative to EI and UCB, respectively. The selected observations improve prediction across six regressor families and provide broader design-space coverage. ROSE-HEA also surpasses the strongest conventional regressor's mean predictive performance using 125 rather than 185 observations, corresponding to 32.4% fewer training observations. These findings demonstrate the potential of outcome-aware robust sequential learning to improve UTS prediction in HEAs under constrained training budgets.


### [75] [Asymptotic Margins and Feature Confinement in Conic Classification Heads under Softmax Cross-Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18507)
*Omer Nezih Gerek*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Classification heads built on conic functions give each class a bounded region of the feature space, and such regions are used to reject samples of unseen classes. We ask whether these regions also keep the features bounded when the head is trained with softmax cross-entropy. For a head whose class scores combine a linear term with a Euclidean norm, we first show that the region of each class is bounded by a quadric of revolution, whose eccentricity is the reciprocal of one learned ratio. We then attach to each class an asymptotic margin, which compares the growth of its score with the growth of all the other scores and can be computed by a second-order cone program. The loss of a class has infimum zero exactly when its margin is positive, and it then approaches zero only along diverging features. When the margin is negative, the loss is coercive and its minimizers lie in an explicit ball. Since softmax depends only on the differences between scores, a class with a bounded region can still have a positive margin, so bounded regions do not confine the features. The result extends to other norms, including the polyhedral conic functions. On CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100, all 1210 cells of 67 trained conic heads have positive margins, whereas a prototype head keeps nine of its ten cells bounded, as the theory predicts.


### [76] [On numerical issues of divergences between nearly equal distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18511)
*Brandon Amos*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Barron et al. (2026) recently identified numerical issues in the softmax and soft cross-entropy loss. We show that similar issues are present in standard divergence operations between categorical distributions. This includes the forward and reverse Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence and generalized Jensen-Shannon divergence (JSD), as well as Monte Carlo KL estimates and entropy. When the distributions are nearly equal, rounding and cancellation issues affect the forward and gradient computations. These issues can affect settings using divergences in optimization objectives, such as knowledge distillation, reinforcement learning, and proximal methods. We propose numerically stable reformulations and provide example implementations for each setting, validated by property-based tests.


### [77] [WeightBench: Evaluating Language Models as Weight-Space Programmers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18553)
*Aayush Marasini, Barsat Khadka, Nick Rahimi*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Recent work shows that large language models (LLMs) can operate directly on the parameters of other neural networks. They can read what a network was trained to do, fix a network that fails, and write working networks from scratch, all without gradient descent. This capability has been termed Weight-Space Programming (WSP). Exploring this new paradigm systematically requires a robust benchmark. We introduce WEIGHTBENCH, a benchmark that evaluates LLMs of increasing capability on 42 WSP tasks across five tracks: reading, fixing and writing weights, recognizing edits that are impossible, and data-free compression. The networks whose weights the LLMs read and edit range from 2K-parameter control policies to 7B-parameter language models. Experimental benchmark results show that frontier agents can already read small networks and write working control policies without ever running them, but cannot yet patch language models, compress them without data, or tell an impossible edit from a hard one. On all 42 tasks, Claude Opus 5.5 reaches a macro score of 0.535 (Sonnet 5.5 0.466, Haiku 5.5 0.402, DeepSeek v4-flash 0.215); on the 38 tasks it was run on, GPT-6.1-sol reaches 0.568 against Opus's 0.546. Code, the task registry, our OS-level sandbox harness, and the live leaderboard are publicly available at this https URL .


### [78] [Pairing AIS with RF: Analysis of a Markovian Approach for Maritime Data Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18563)
*Daniel Grimm, Kyra Jongman, Renato Cordeiro Ferreira, Giuseppe Cascavilla, Alfredo Cuzzocrea, Willem-Jan Van Den Heuvel*

Main category: cs.LG

Abstract: Monitoring maritime activities is important for safety and security, particularly for detecting illegal activities, but remains challenging due to the size and complexity of the maritime domain. The Automatic Identification System (AIS) is commonly used for this purpose, despite its known vulnerabilities. In contrast, space-based radio frequency (RF) observations provide an independent source of vessel activity but lack ship identification and therefore cannot identify vessels on their own. Following the CRISP-DM process, this study applies a Pair Hidden Markov Model (PHMM) to align individual RF detections with AIS trajectories, using temporal and spatial information while modeling noise and timing discrepancies. We evaluate it on NOAA AIS data with synthetic RF detections against nearest-neighbor baselines and analyze its errors with bias tests on trajectory length, sampling density, stationarity and AIS--RF distance. The length-corrected Forward score reaches a top-1 precision of $0.7776$ on the validation split of multi-candidate detections, where errors are possible, and an AUC of $0.637$ for detecting dark vessels among the detections that pass the prefilter. With a single RF detection, the score mostly reflects the AIS trajectory itself, and its errors favor longer and more stationary trajectories. A log-odds score, which compares the full model with a null model without match transitions, raises precision to $0.8580$ and AUC to $0.852$. Its length-corrected variant no longer shows a significant bias towards stationary trajectories and short inter-message distances, but still favors candidates farther from the RF detection. A time-weighted nearest-neighbor baseline without fitting remains ahead (precision $0.9013$, AUC $0.904$), so this single-detection, hand-designed PHMM formulation approaches but does not beat it.

# cs.DC [[Back]](#toc)

### [79] [Performance-Portable Exact Gaussian Process Regression in Python via SYCL and oneMath](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17565)
*Alexander Strack, Marcel Graf, Alexander Van Craen, Dirk Pflüger*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Modern multi-vendor GPU environments force a choice between portable code and native performance. GPRat is an open-source Python library for exact Gaussian process regression built on the HPX asynchronous many-task runtime, tiled algorithms, and CPU/CUDA BLAS backends. We contribute a UXL Foundation oneMath backend with a hybrid HPX/SYCL design, extending GPRat to AMD and Intel GPUs. The new SYCL backend comes within 3% of native CUDA on an NVIDIA A30 at the largest problem size and outperforms the GPflow and GPyTorch libraries by up to 1.75x and 2.17x on the A30 and up to 1.79x and 4.62x on an AMD Instinct MI210. On an Intel Arc B580, a consumer-class card with FP64 throughput below our CPUs, the unmodified SYCL backend trails GPRat's CPU backend. Overall, oneMath's dispatch to vendor-native BLAS lets a single SYCL codebase match CUDA on NVIDIA and outperform established Python GP frameworks on AMD, without vendor-specific code.


### [80] [Superfluid: Local AI Server for Distributed LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17753)
*Prabod Rathnayaka, Isura Manchanayake, Srikandabala Kogul, Steve Siu, Fabian Waschkowski, Lukas Wesemann*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Running large language models (LLMs) on local devices keeps data private, removes per-token cost, cuts network latency, and keeps working without a connection. Two shifts now strain how such models are served. On one device, several agents share a locally served model with an interactive user. Across devices, a developer or small team often owns several capable machines from different vendors, which sit idle most of the day because every local server serves one runtime on one machine. We present Superfluid, an open-source LLM server built for local inference at both scales. On one device, Superfluid drives this http URL , MLX, or BaseRT in isolated worker processes through one scheduler that moves interactive requests ahead of agent work, computes a prompt shared by many requests once, and writes every token to a durable log before it reaches the client. Across devices, one Superfluid server turns a fleet of machines into a single API endpoint. Machines with different hardware serve copies of the same model, receive work according to their measured speed, and can join or fail under load without losing a request. For Qwen3.8-27B on an Apple M5 Pro, a chat sent while eight agents are working receives its first token in 1.5 s, against 291-612 s on llama-server, Ollama, and this http URL . On six machines from three chip vendors serving 64 simulated users for one hour, Superfluid answers all 5,041 requests while machines join one at a time and one is killed and rejoins; throughput grows from 112 to 534 tok/s and the median first-token time falls from 99 to 12 s. On twelve Mac Studio M3 Ultras it sustains 10,656 tok/s across 38,563 requests from 384 agents without a failure. Superfluid is available under the Apache-2.0 licence at this https URL .


### [81] [TailGuard-GDS: Tail-Safe Control of GPU Storage Data Movement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17824)
*Jiaqi Liu, Fan Yang, Xiaohang Shi, Xianzhe Zhang, Tao Jiang, Guojun Yuan, Zhan Wang, Guangming Tan*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: AI runtimes move state between NVMe storage and GPU memory on critical paths. GPUDirect Storage (GDS) removes host staging, yet our eight-H100 measurements show it is not consistently tail-optimal for semantically identical GPU-buffer operations. The preferred path varies with operation, request size, concurrency, placement, and resource contention; bandwidth and tail-latency winners can disagree. Frameworks specify what moves and when, while GPU I/O stacks expose mechanisms; neither combines phase semantics with measured tail behavior to choose a delivery plan. We present TailGuard-GDS, a runtime that selects a semantically equivalent plan per application phase. It profiles plans varying backend, request shape, and placement, minimizes GPU-visible tail latency under phase constraints, and revisits decisions as conditions change. Resource reuse keeps policy lookup and switching at microsecond-scale overhead. Integrated with PyTorch FSDP and DeepSpeed ZeRO-3, TailGuard reduces FSDP step p95 by 18.4% and GPU-idle p95 by 25.3% versus the strongest fixed path. Across five eight-GPU ZeRO-3 workloads, step p95 falls 7.9-24.6% versus fixed host staging and 28.4-42.6% versus fixed GDS. Against tuned DeepSpeed AIO, TailGuard reduces step p95 by 5.6% and raises estimated throughput by 6.1%.


### [82] [OrbitWeaver: LLM Agent Planning with Simulation Feedback for Optically Interconnected Orbital GPU Clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17850)
*Pengcheng Luo, Bowen Zhang*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Space-based data-center designs increasingly consider GPU computing formations connected by inter-satellite optical links. With limited neighboring connections, jobs assigned disjoint GPUs can still contend on shared relay paths. Rank placement and communication priorities jointly affect this contention and the readiness of downstream computation. We present OrbitWeaver, an offline large language model (LLM) agent planner that jointly optimizes rank placement and communication order. Guided by communication structure, execution dependencies, and simulation feedback, the agent constructs and refines plans that coordinate shared-link access with computation progress. This coordination reduces contention and dependency blocking, shortening mean job completion time (JCT). Simulation experiments on optically interconnected orbital GPU clusters show that OrbitWeaver reduces aggregate mean JCT by 6.2-33.5% relative to the state-of-the-art baselines CASSINI, Crux, and Symphony. These results demonstrate the value of LLM-guided planning for efficient concurrent computing in orbital GPU clusters.


### [83] [The Exact Stabilization Time of Self-Stabilizing Coloring: Mean, Variance, and Distribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17989)
*{É}lie de Panafieu, Fabien Mathieu, Maria Gradinariu Potop-Butucaru, S{é}bastien Tixeuil*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: The worst-case stabilization time of a self-stabilizing algorithm usually says little about its typical behavior beyond the order of magnitude. This paper revisits the classical self-stabilizing $Delta+1$ coloring algorithm with vertices activated at random, one at a time (the random central daemon). Considering the number of color changes as a random variable, we study its mean, variance and, on several families of graphs, its exact distribution. The analysis rests on a reduction: a vertex changes color, at most once, exactly when it is not a local maximum of a uniformly random labeling of the vertices, maxima being taken among the neighbors that share its initial color (the monochrome initial configuration is the slowest case). What can be predicted depends on what is known of the graph. The degree distribution (the view at distance one) gives the mean. The variance needs the view at distance two, in particular the triangles, and the full law needs the whole graph. We give closed-form results, from monochrome and from uniformly random initial configurations, on paths, cycles, random trees, Erdős-R{é}nyi graphs and bipartite random graphs. From a monochrome start, the mean stays within a factor two of the worst case, whereas the variance ranges from zero (disjoint cliques) to order $n^2$ (the star on $n$ vertices)..


### [84] [Zero-SM NCCL: A Systematic Guide to Designing and Deploying SM-Free GPU Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18075)
*Zhenhao He, Siyuan Shen, Ke Wen, Prajwal Singhania, Shiqing Fan, Youngeun Kwon, Sangkug Lym, Vasudevan Rengasamy, Vishnuswaroop Ramesh, Yicheng Qiang, Craig Belusar, Jeff Hammond, Torsten Hoefler*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: GPU communication libraries such as NCCL use Streaming Multiprocessors (SMs) for data movement and synchronization, slowing overlapped computation at a cost that grows with interconnect bandwidth. We present Zero-SM NCCL, which communicates within and across scale-up domains without launching any SM kernel. It synchronizes through CUDA stream memory operations, moves data asynchronously with copy engines within a scale-up domain, and drives network RDMA from a stream-ordered CPU proxy. It supports data-movement collectives such as AllGather and AlltoAll, plus one-sided ncclPutSignal and ncclWaitSignal APIs. On GB200 NVL72 and B200 NVL8 systems, Zero-SM collectives reach up to 1.36x higher AllGather bandwidth than NCCL's legacy SM-based kernels and cut the median slowdown of overlapped GEMMs from 33% to 4%, at a higher latency per operation. In Megatron-LM training, they accelerate FSDP by up to 3.5%, and one-sided puts accelerate pipeline parallelism by up to 15%. Zero-SM communication is not a universal win, however. Through an end-to-end study across models and parallelism strategies, we show that it pays off for large transfers in GPU-bound workloads and for deep pipelines with large bubbles, but loses on small messages and host-bound workloads. We distill these findings into guidelines for enabling zero-SM communication in NCCL and building it into other libraries.


### [85] [Multi-GPU Tridiagonal Partition Algorithm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18111)
*Milena Veneva*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: This paper presents a multi-GPU version and a multi-GPU recursive version of the parallel partition method for systems of linear algebraic equations with tridiagonal coefficient matrices and their implementations in CUDA. Computational experiments on one and two GPU cards, for different system sizes and with different number of recursive steps are conducted. The results are analyzed and discussed.


### [86] [Compiling Specialized GPU-Initiated Networking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18145)
*Javid Baydamirli, Ismayil Ismayilov, Kaan Oktay, Didem Unat*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Machine learning systems specialize computation to the workload and hardware, but communication remains in generic libraries whose device code and transport decisions are not visible to high-level compilers. We present Obaleth, a compiler that makes GPU-initiated communication part of a kernel's intermediate representation, co-optimizes it alongside computation, and lowers it to specialized transport code. Obaleth plans completions around buffer lifetimes and optimizes memory ordering, synchronization, and batching across NVIDIA and AMD GPUs. On H100 GPUs, Obaleth sends small messages up to 3.3$\times$ faster, and on 256 GPUs runs all-to-all 1.25$\times$ and 2.3$\times$ faster than NVSHMEM and NCCL GIN, with much smaller memory and register use. In vLLM, it serves Qwen3-235B on 16 B200 GPUs and answers requests up to 1.29$\times$ faster than with DeepEP.


### [87] [BPU-SpikeMM:Exact Matrix Multiplication via Spatio-Temporal Spike Encoding on Real Neuromorphic Hardware](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18275)
*Rongyuan Guo, Yufeng Zhang, Haotian Wang, Canming Yao, Yuxiang Huan, Wangdong Yang, Kenli Li*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Matrix multiplication is the dominant computational kernel in deep learning, yet its growing scale strains conventional von Neumann hardware in energy efficiency and bandwidth. Neuromorphic architectures offer a promising alternative, but mathematical equivalence between matrix multiplication and spiking computation does not guarantee direct execution on real neuromorphic hardware, as the specific hardware paradigm may still require additional data recovery, bit-weight reconstruction, or state readout after neuromorphic execution. This paper presents BPU-SpikeMM (Brain Processing Unit-based Spike Matrix Multiplication), an exact spatio-temporal spike encoding framework for matrix multiplication on real neuromorphic hardware. We prove the exact equivalence of spike-encoded matrix multiplication under the BPU execution paradigm, distinguish mathematical equivalence from native direct-readout equivalence on BPU hardware, and build a spatio-temporal offloading pipeline supporting multiple encoding schemes. Furthermore, we extend the framework to a multi-dimensional encoding design space and evaluate the applicability of different encodings on real BPU hardware in terms of correctness, temporal resource cost, spatial resource cost, and event-driven metrics. The results confirm native direct-readout equivalence across all evaluated encoding schemes and show that their applicability to BPU execution depends on joint trade-offs among temporal cost, spatial resource consumption, and event activity.


### [88] [Callo: Serving Cache-Miss Requests with Speculative Decoding on Invalidated KV under Prefill-Decode Disaggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18459)
*Jianjun Ou, Matthew Lentz*

Main category: cs.DC

Abstract: Agent sessions may involve frequent context edits, which effectively invalidate cached KV state and force requests to wait for re-prefill before decoding. We present Callo, a disaggregated serving system that leverages invalidated KV for speculative drafts to reduce this latency, while verifying drafts against the exact KV after prefill before committing tokens. Callo makes effective use of decode resources by scheduling speculations during periods of underutilization and preempting speculations when drafts are likely to not be accepted. We evaluated Callo on a two-GPU testbed using SWE-agent sessions across three model families, observing all-request speedups of 1.05-1.19x over vLLM at moderate load. We demonstrate that these gains are also realized at scale through cluster-level simulations driven by real-world traces.


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [89] [When Should LLM Agents Forget? Memory Filtering Under Query-Critical Resolver Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17338)
*Samarth Vinayaka*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: LLM memory systems often delete facts classified as stale. We show that this hard-filtering step can reduce downstream accuracy when resolver errors delete query-critical facts. Across three models, a controlled sensitivity intervention moves hard-drop from gains of +0.186 to +0.230 at perfect resolution to losses of -0.176 to -0.181 at the lowest tested reliability. Paired targeted interventions show that false-drop causes significantly greater degradation than false-keep, while a non-deleting baseline can still fail when incorrect labels steer generation. On a frozen 102/102 calibration/test split, a simple deployment-matched gate reaches 0.655 accuracy versus 0.641 for always hard-drop and 0.662 for test-informed oracle selection. Global-accuracy crossovers require gold labels and are model- and task-specific diagnostics, not deployment thresholds. Deletion should instead be treated as cost-sensitive: validate query-critical false-drop risk on matched data, or default to label-free retention when such validation is unavailable.


### [90] [Does the Ranking Hold? A Practitioner-Facing Audit of Hallucination Detectors on Small, Quantized, On-Device LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17339)
*Shubhra Mittal, Gaurav Naresh Mittal*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Hallucination detection for large language models is dominated by methods evaluated on 7B-70B research models with abundant sampling budgets and exact-match or ROUGE correctness labels. The small models practitioners actually ship on-device, Phi-3-mini, Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, and Gemma-2-2B-Instruct, typically at 4-bit precision, sit outside this evidence base. We present a reproducible one-GPU audit of four detectors spanning three paradigms: length-normalized token-margin calibration, a lexical n-gram baseline, and two sampling-based consistency methods (semantic entropy and SelfCheckGPT-NLI) on two small open instruction-tuned models (Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct) loaded at 4-bit NF4 quantization on a single Colab T4 GPU, evaluated on closed-book TriviaQA. Cross-model ranking transfer is poor: Spearman rho is -0.80 and Kendall tau-b is -0.667, meaning the detector ranking actively inverts between the two models, and the cheap lexical baseline is statistically indistinguishable from the sampling-based consistency detectors on both models under bootstrap 95 percent confidence intervals. As a second, methodological contribution, we show that the standard token-overlap-to-multiple-choice correctness labeler for TruthfulQA-Generation produces near-chance AUROC (0.46-0.59) for all four detectors on both models, a labeler failure, not a detector failure, since the same detectors reach 0.69-0.89 AUROC on TriviaQA under the identical pipeline. We release the harness, prompts, and per-item scores and frame the detector choice explicitly as a fairness-of-error-rates allocation problem for practitioners deploying on resource-constrained, on-device hardware.


### [91] [LoRAWeaver: Directional Adapter Routing in LoRA Representation Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17340)
*Inish Melroy D Souza, Tom Jackson, Vitor Castro, Rebecca Higginson, Andrea Soltoggio*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Large language models are expensive to update through full retraining. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning reduces the cost of adaptation, but repeated adaptation creates a growing pool of task-specific LoRA adapters. This pool complicates adapter selection during inference. Existing routing and encoder-based methods often require retraining when new adapters are added, which limits their use in dynamic settings. This paper treats adapter routing as a representation alignment problem in the adapter-output space. The proposed training-free method selects adapters by matching adapter-conditioned query representations against task prototypes using cosine similarity. Experiments with LLaMA-2-7B and LLaMA-2-13B on a 48-task FLAN-v2 benchmark and on out-of-distribution tasks show that the method outperforms training-free baselines in the cross-task setting and approaches a trained retrieval encoder without learning a router. An ablation confirms the benefit of directional alignment in the adapter-output space.


### [92] [Tabular Population Priors as Structured Retrieval for EHR Medication Set Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17341)
*Animesh Agarwal, Meysam Ghaffari, Nina Fatehi, Carlos Morato*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Medication prediction from electronic health records is a fixed vocabulary structured prediction problem, yet recent clinical RAG systems often treat it like open ended case based reasoning: retrieve similar patient admissions and ask an LLM to infer a medication set. This retrieval object is indirect the model receives retrieved patient records while the target is a discrete set of medication class labels. We propose a more task aligned retrieval object: a tabular population prior ranked medication class candidates from a sparse condition medication co occurrence table estimated from training admissions. Using PACERAG as a fixed downstream pipeline and replacing only its patient retriever, this drop in swap improves F1 from 0.303 to 0.338 (+11.5% relative) and Jaccard from 0.218 to 0.237 on 1,004 heldout MIMIC IV admissions, with no additional LLM calls and no inference time patient index. A non LLM top k baseline reaches F1=0.248, confirming the prior is predictive before LLM integration. Aggregate population statistics can provide a more task aligned, efficient, and auditable retrieval source than patient level nearest neighbors for fixed vocabulary EHR set prediction.


### [93] [An Operationalization of Opaque Serial Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17495)
*Nathan Sheffield, Alek Westover, Lukas Finnveden, Alexa Pan, Julian Stastny, Ryan Greenblatt*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) is currently a valuable tool for overseeing AI models, but some architectural shifts could significantly reduce CoT monitorability. We operationalize a measure that serves as a proxy for the amount of unverbalized serial cognition a model can perform, as a specific instantiation of the notion of opaque serial depth: roughly, the length of the longest path through a model's computation that does not pass through an interpretable bottleneck. Our main contribution is a particular standard for what counts as such a bottleneck. We define natural-language-rooted (NL-rooted) nodes as nodes that were initialized from a pre-training prior to produce human-understandable outputs, whose output space has not been expanded beyond that prior, and whose outputs the model has not been modified to interpret as a different type of data. By construction, the resulting measure, NLS depth, can be adjudicated before training from an architecture, training recipe, and deployment configuration alone, and is insensitive to ordinary post-training such as prompting, distillation, and RL. We find that, across open-weight models, NLS depth correlates strongly with layer count, with some exceptions, and has grown slowly over recent years. It would likely grow slowly under continued scaling of standard transformers, while latent-reasoning architectures such as full-bandwidth transformers could increase it by a large amount. We work through how NLS depth applies to a range of existing and proposed architectures, discuss alternative operationalizations, and give a worked example bounding the depth of Gemma3-1B. We argue that AI developers should track and report the NLS depth of their models as a complement to direct measurements of CoT monitorability.


### [94] [Advancing Quantization for R&R Models with Microscaling Formats](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17501)
*Yavuz Yetim, Bor-Yiing Su, Zhe Wang, Arthur Liu, Pranav Sharma, Ying Wang, Randy Shuai, Luke Pfister, Ruichao Xiao, Bill Zhao, Zihao Liu, Liyuan Li, Jiangyuan Li, Gaoxiang Liu, Nathan Yan, Geonhwa Jeong, Jatin Chhugani, Elias Ellison, Uladzimir Pashkevich*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Recommendation and Ranking (R&R) models, particularly in digital advertising, require massive inference throughput but exhibit extreme sensitivity to precision loss. Unlike Large Language Models (LLMs) that frequently tolerate quantization-induced accuracy drops of 1% or more, production-grade R&R models enforce a stringent launch bar: model quality must remain within a 0.01% Normalized Entropy (NE) margin relative to the baseline, which commonly uses FP16 or FP32. This tight constraint has historically hindered the adoption of aggressive quantization in this domain. In this paper, we present an empirical evaluation of both MXFP8 and the 6-bit MXFP6 format for industrial-scale R&R inference on the AMD MI350X architecture. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first paper, across R&R and LLM domains, that reports significant speedups from native MXFP6 GEMMs, which quantize both weights and activations and compute directly on 6-bit elements, without sacrificing model quality. We demonstrate that we can selectively apply this 6-bit format to maximize overall QPS gain without breaching the strict NE parity threshold. We further show that MXFP8 is a viable alternative to row-wise FP8: the two formats have nearly the same accuracy on our models, so the faster kernel can be chosen for each GEMM shape. On two production ads ranking models, our implementation shows measured GEMM kernel speedups over FP16 of 1.33--1.55x for MXFP8 and 1.73--1.99x for MXFP6 across all supported linear layers, and up to 2.90x for MXFP6 on individual layers, alongside end-to-end (E2E) inference throughput gains from selective MXFP6 of 1.5--5.3% for Model A and 7.6--14.3% for Model B over their FP8 configurations.


### [95] [A Push Down the Mountain: Framing Effects and Internal State Interventions on Collusive Behavior in a Long-Horizon LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17515)
*Harrison C. Margolin*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Can sustained gain-loss (valence) framing sway an LLM agent toward market manipulation? We place Gemma 3 12B into the seat of a defendant in the London silver-fixing litigation, in a market simulation built from real case transcripts. Across 260 simulations, the agent is asked to collude on the price of silver by a counterparty. We draw from behavioral economics to vary the counterparty's requests across four categorical frames: gain, loss, blame and credit. We also add pressure from the agent's management, as well as endorsement by another defendant. In Phase II, we extract the internal states induced by texts evoking each frame and rerun our experiment with those states injected at the model's layer 22. In Phase I, loss framing raised participation to 25% (p = 0.007), and blame to 33% (p = 0.0003), compared to only 5% under neutral framing. A second bank's endorsement raised participation under credit (praise) from 18% to 40% (Holm p = 0.027). Pressure from management raised trading volume 10-19 times but did not raise collusion. In Phase II, injected states pointed the other way. Loss and blame states made the agent more vigilant, refusing all 60 requests (p < 0.001). Gain and credit states raised collusion to 32% from 13% with no state (p = 0.051). At one saved decision, when UBS asked directly to sell through an identified stop-loss, injected states led the agent to sell in 37 of 40 replies, versus 1 of 10 with no state and 0 of 10 with a random state of the same strength.


### [96] [Model Annealing: Data-Efficient Learning through Review and Consolidation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17524)
*Ali Mohammadi Ruzbahani, Rezvan Abouzadeh*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: A small labelled dataset is memorized long before the training budget runs out, and the one-hot labels that remain to be fitted then mostly sharpen confidence. Model annealing spends the rest of the budget on review and consolidation, relearning from the network's own knowledge. Training is divided into episodes whose learning rate is raised again and cooled, every cooled state joins a memory of past selves, and each new episode learns from the labels, from the tempered predictions of this memory, and from adversarial dreams sought where student and memory disagree most. The memory averages predictions rather than weights, and the deployed network is the last student, so inference costs nothing extra. Across CIFAR-100, Tiny-ImageNet and Imagewoof, four architecture families and 5\% to 100\% of the labels, model annealing raises the top-1 accuracy of the AdamW-trained ConvNeXt, ViT and MLP-Mixer over cross-entropy training in 28 of 33 configurations, by 1.3 points on average, and is on par in accuracy with snapshot distillation, the closest competitor. On these families it is better than both in likelihood, calibration and robustness to corruptions and small adversarial perturbations. For an SGD-trained ResNet-18 it lowers accuracy but still improves robustness to small adversarial perturbations. Separating the reheated schedule from relearning shows that relearning helps every family and that the losses come from reheats, which undo most of this network's fit to its training set. How early the accuracy of a cross-entropy run saturates largely separates the AdamW families from ResNet-18 but does not predict the gain within them. With little data, relearning from the latest snapshot without dreams, as snapshot distillation does, compounds overconfidence in the AdamW families from episode to episode. The memory and the dreams each curb most of this drift, and together they remove it.


### [97] [Astrocyte Biocomputing: Astrocytic Contributions to Neural Network Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17532)
*Kate M. O'Neill, Ian Whitehouse, Adam N. McCaughan, Ryan O'Loughlin, Sonia Buckley, Wolfgang Losert*

Main category: cs.AI

Abstract: Conductors are critical for the performance of an orchestra even though they do not emit sound. Basic research and clinical studies in the last decade are revealing that astrocytes may be the "conductors" of the "orchestra" that is the brain network: astrocytes make up approximately $50\%$ of all brain cells but do not emit electrical signals. Nevertheless, they may play critical roles in important cognitive brain functions, specifically learning, memory, and information processing. Here we consider the implications of these discoveries for brain-inspired machine learning (ML), where analogy is typically drawn between neurons and their electrical interactions. We highlight specific algorithms for two key astrocyte characteristics. The first characteristic is the ability to link to and modulate thousands of synapses simultaneously. A model based on this characteristic yields faster training and makes the network more robust to noise, providing a supervised learning algorithm that can be applied to any existing neural network. The second characteristic we focus on is that the non-electrical communication among astrocytes includes slow, rhythmic dynamics. The rhythmic sharing algorithm we developed based on this premise yields rapid anomaly detection adaptation and prevents catastrophic forgetting.
